## Global Thematic Strategy

Key Upcoming Elections, Equity Implications, Policy-Linked Stock Ideas

The global election cycle is just warming up, with 60 of 77 elections still remaining across key geographies including the US, UK, India, Mexico and South Africa (see Report). While macro themes remain dominant, focus is beginning to center on the US Presidential and Congressional elections given the policy differences between the leading candidates, the size of the US equity market, and the potential knock-on effects to other geographies, particularly in EM. In this report, we explore various election scenarios and provide 11 key thematic stock baskets, 11 stock screens and sector/style recommendations that align with major policy proposals across geographies (see Figure 1 for full list of themes). Further, we preview key upcoming elections and their implications across DM and EM, including the US, UK, India, Mexico and South Africa (see detailed breakdown below).

US Elections (Nov 5). In the US, a loss for the incumbent could drive substantial changes in areas such as trade policy (e.g. US-China), foreign policy (e.g., Middle East/Israel, Russia/Ukraine, etc.), regulation (e.g. environment/energy, anti-trust) and fiscal/tax policy (e.g. TCJA sunset on 12/31/25). The fact that such a high-stake election cannot be called at this point in time should be some cause of concern for equity investors looking to manage exposure to key US policy themes. While betting odds and polling provide some real time visibility into the outcome, there remains significant uncertainty and room for a November surprise given how narrow the leads are for either candidate and for both chambers of Congress. Especially for Presidential elections, the result will likely be driven by tens of thousands of votes, not millions, across 4-5 swing states. In our view, we view gridlock as the most neutral outcome in the short/ medium term, as it would limit changes to existing policies passed under the current administration and limit the implementation of new, and possibly volatile ones, though risks remain around unilateral executive and/or agency action. For a full breakdown of key scenarios, see US Equity Strategy. For a full breakdown of key thematic policy equity baskets constructed in collaboration with JPM stock analysts, see Figure 19 to Figure 28. For JPM proprietary NLP toolbased stock screens on US politics, Trade policy and China based on corporate and news sentiment, see Figure 40 to Figure 45.

UK Elections (before 1/28/25). Labour is maintaining a sizeable lead in the current election polling over the incumbent conservatives. In the last elections held in 2019, the general consensus was that a Labour victory would be a negative for the overall equity market. There were concerns around nationalization of key industries and also the possibility of higher taxes. In our assessment, the current Labour leadership under Kier Starmer is much more centrist than the manifesto going into the 2019 elections would suggest. They have already distanced themselves from much of the Corbyn Era nationalization promises, with the exception of the railways, for which there appears to be broad public support. We do not see them taking a big-bang approach towards the economy given fiscal constraints, but rather focusing on slow-moving supply-side reforms that encourage private investment. Nevertheless, a victory for Labour would likely be

India General Election (19 April - 1 June 2024). Historically heading into elections, we find: (1) as elections get going, the market goes through an extended period of elevated volatility that declines sharply post conclusion; (2) Nifty has delivered +9%/+8% in the 3/6 months post general elections since 1991, which means any correction or dip usually ends up as a buying opportunity. Based on latest opinion polls, BJP-led NDA remains firmly in the pole position for the 2024 national elections. Key states to watch will be Uttar Pradesh, Maharashtra, West Bengal and Bihar, which send 210 of 543 members of parliament to the Lok Sabha. If the ruling party retains power in the 2024 elections with an in-line/higher-thanexpected seat share, this should lead to broad economic and fiscal policy continuity. We believe real estate, EMS and capex recovery plays like industrials, utilities, cement, auto and banks will be key beneficiaries. (see India Equity Strategy for full details).

Mexico General Election (June 2<sup>nd</sup>). Although we believe the election is unlikely to be a big market mover, there is some room from positive surprises should the race be closer than what current polls are showing. With the incumbent party polling around 60%, on average (\~30pp gap to the opposition), all eyes are on whether it will be able to reach the two-thirds majority in Congress required to pass constitutional reforms, which we believe is not currently priced in and implies a downside risk. However, it is important to note that the opposition coalition has been gaining some momentum at the state level, which ultimately has important implications on the Congressional composition. In addition, as is the case every 12 years, Mexico<sup>’</sup>s election cycle coincides with the US<sup>’</sup>s, whose results, and its read-through towards Mexico, seem to be of far greater concern for investors than the domestic story. We provide a list of 10 OW- or N-rated names that have historically outperformed following an election regardless of the outcome (see Mexico Equity Strategy for full details).

South Africa General Election (May 29<sup>th</sup> ). The 29 May elections/June potential coalition formation process are much discussed by SA investors, but we see little (c 2-4%) downside risk priced into SA equities. On a bottom-up sector basis, we see few policy risks. Right now we see the ANC likely to achieve 45% – as best we can tell from small number of high variance polls; we will update views as we get new polling data. That puts some market uncertainty into the formation of a post-election coalition, which on these numbers seems the most likely outcome. We see upside risks too – an investor-friendly coalition could spark a rally, like the 24%/two-month rally we saw in the Dec <sup>‘</sup>17-Feb <sup>‘</sup>18 Ramaphoria. Our key OW sectors on the JSE include General Miners and Gold among the offshore sectors and Banks and Staples among the domestic sectors; our key UWs are General Retail and Food Producers. We are structurally UW South Africa in our CEEMEA and EM country allocation until we see the “two twos<sup>”</sup>: two years of >2% GDP growth, which should trigger a domestic re-rating and better earnings growth. However, near term, we see SA equities as oversold and likely to bounce ahead of elections.

## US Equity Strategy

Lock, Top, Surge, Tail. Unlike past election cycles, when the debate focused on which set of policies are more positive for equity markets, the current scenarios seem to be more neutral, with the potential for meaningful negative outcomes. An incumbent Presidential victory would likely be more of the same given a continued gridlocked Congress limiting the passage of new transformative policies. We have already seen the impact of this political gridlock given the difficulty in passing new legislation since the 2022 midterms (see Figure 3). Therefore, investors should focus on managing the risks of a Republican President (with/without a Republican Congressional majority), as it would be a more meaningful departure from the status quo. It is worth highlighting that both candidates continue to be hawkish on China without a clear path back to normalized pre-US/China trade war relations, though the methods of pursuing trade policy differ (e.g., executive orders/export controls vs. tariffs, etc., see Report). Extraneous factors such as third-party runs (i.e., Independent candidates) will likely tilt the election at the margin, especially in key swing states, but a successful third-party challenge to the existing two-party system remains a low probability outcome due to its narrow road to victory (i.e., eligibility in state ballots, failure of either major candidate reaching 270 and election in the House, which will vote by State delegation).

Bluelock Scenario. A victory by the incumbent Democratic party in the Presidential election is likely the most market-neutral scenario and cements key legislative achievements of the past few years, including the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law, Inflation Reduction Act and CHIPS and Science Act. The latter two should continue to drive de-globalization/reshoring. A victory by the incumbent party for the Presidency is likely to be accompanied by a gridlocked Congress or outright Republican Congress, but with narrow majorities. Under a split Congress scenario, follow-on legislation to the CHIPS and Science Act, Child Tax Credit expansion, changes to corporate R&D deductibility, and industrial policy addressing Critical Minerals could still be passed on a bipartisan basis. The incumbent could also look to prioritize policy initiatives on trade, anti-trust and student debt, which could be actioned by executive authority alone (e.g., recent Steel/Aluminum tariff proposal). Looking at the implications of gridlock on the longer term, provisions of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (TCJA) that affect individual income tax rates, Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT) exemptions and certain business tax cuts are automatically expiring at the end of 2025 (see Report). Democrats have indicated that they would not want to fund permanent individual tax rates by hiking taxes on those making > \$400k. If a divided government is not able to act in a bipartisan manner to make these provisions permanent, this could present an incremental fiscal headwind for US households (e.g., individuals paying AMT jump from \~200K to \~7.6M, see Tax Policy Center) and corporates. Note, statutory corporate taxes were made permanent within the TCJA. It is plausible that an increase in corporate taxes could be used to fund a more permanent cut in individual taxes/other corporate provisions, but this would require Republican support. Overall, the Joint Committee on Taxation (JCT) estimates that making permanent either expiring or less generous provisions after the sunset would cost \~\$3.5T (see CRS). For investors looking to manage exposure to De-Globalization/Re-shoring (JPAMDGLO <Index>), the CHIPS and Science Act (JPAMCHIP <Index>), Child Tax Credit Beneficiaries (JPAMCTCB (<Index), see Figure 27, Figure 20 and Figure 26 for companies sensitive to these themes. For companies that could be sensitive to anti-trust, see Report page 6.

Figure 3: Current Congressional Gridlock Especially Pronounced Versus Past 50 Years

Red Top Scenario. A Red Top scenario in which the Republican challenger wins the Presidency with divided Congress would be impactful to the market through likely changes in US trade policy, foreign policy, energy policy and additional scrutiny as it relates to anti-trust (e.g., media companies). As it relates to trade, these changes could manifest in the form of raising existing tariffs on China to 60%, imposing a 10% universal tariff on all other trading partners and/or a 100% tariff on auto production in Mexico by Chinese automakers, which would have significant implications to US equities across sectors/styles (see here and here).The 10% tariff alone could raise \~\$300B in revenue (see Tax Foundation) but would be offset by second and third order effects of their implementation. These areas are largely governed by the executive and can be influenced without explicit congressional approval. While still unclear, changes to US foreign policy on Russia/Ukraine and the Middle East could shift perceived geopolitical risks in both regions. This could be especially true in the case of Iran, where more hawkish responses have been advocated by Republicans. Lastly, as it relates to permitting reform, a Red Top scenario could see significant barriers lifted by selling more federal land and eliminating climate regulations. For investors looking to manage exposure to escalating US-China tariffs, see US companies with high direct revenue exposure to China (JPAMCREV <Index>) and US Importers (JPAMUSIM <Index>). For energy names well positioned for elevated Geopolitical risk such as Iran, see JPAMGNRG <Index>. For energy names that could benefit from permitting reform, see JPAMPERM <Index>. Full company constituents can be found in Figure 19, Figure 28, Figure 21 and Figure 22.

● Red Surge Scenario. A Red Surge scenario would unlock the legislative pen for Republicans, who are likely to retake the Senate with the possibility of keeping expanding their majority in the House (discussed above). Key legislative achievements under the current administration would face scrutiny, such as provisions of the Inflation Reduction Act. Some corporates have indicated that given the amount of funds going to Red states, they are not expecting a significant change in IRA spending, but perhaps a slowdown (see Report). The slowdown would likely be tied to the open-ended provisions such as tax credit bonus adders (see Report). Moreover, on tax policy, a Red Surge could open the door for permanent individual tax cuts that are sunsetting at the end of 2025. Further, the statutory corporate tax rate would be maintained (currently 21%, previously 35% pre-TCJA passage in 2017), with the potential for new business incentives/subsidies funded by proceeds from the tariff increases. While many of the actions viable under the Red Top Scenario remain valid under a Red Surge Scenario, an additional option for trade policy would be the repeal of permanent normal trade relations (PNTR) with China, which would require congressional support. A repeal of PNTR could be another course for influencing US-China trade but would require a mitigation strategy for the potential detrimental impact if actioned alone (see US-China Business Council, Cato Institute, Heritage Foundation, American Action Forum). Lastly, while no clear position has yet been staked out by Republicans regarding Defense spending, rising geopolitical tensions could trigger calls for additional outlays, especially for domestic infrastructure. This would be in the context of existing issues with keeping Ukraine supplied with munitions and resumption of great power security competition. A risk to much of the Republican agenda would be the difficulty Republicans have faced in managing narrow majorities within their own party (e.g., debt ceiling deal/vote of no confidence for House Speak Kevin McCarthy). For investors looking to manage exposure, see baskets associated with the Red Top scenario, along with Green/EV/IRA (JPAMIRAG <Index>) and US Govt

Spending (JPAMGOVT), see Figure 23 and Figure 25, respectively, for companies sensitive to these themes.

Blue Wave Tail Scenario. Given it is still 6 months away from the election (11/5), the possibility remains for Democrats to successfully manage a second Presidential term, retain control of the Senate, and win back the House. The difficulties for Democrats retaining Senate control limits this outcome, but the equity market impact to the headline policies would likely be neutral to negative. However, it is worth exploring the potential policy outcomes. As highlighted in the State of the Union, were Democrats to regain the majority, key policy shifts could include further de-globalization/onshoring initiatives through government subsidies and regulation (e.g. IRA/Chips 2.0), raising taxes, and more directly addressing geopolitical concerns. As it relates to taxes, we could expect: 1) raising the corporate tax rate to 28% from 21%; 2) imposing 15% corporate minimum tax for those making more than \$1B in earnings over the past 3 years; 3) raising the current buyback tax of 1% to 4%; 4) raising the top marginal tax rate to 39.6% from 37% currently for those making \$400K (individual) or \$450K (married); 5) imposing 25% wealth tax on total income (including unrealized cap gains) of >\$100M; 6) closing the carried interest loophole by taxing capital gains as regular income for those earning more than \$1M. There has also been mention of raising the top capital gains rate to 44.6%. Revenues raised could drive a material expansion in child tax credits. IRA provisions for healthcare could also be expanded to 500 drugs over the next decade. Lastly, additional funding could be made available to Ukraine/Israel going forward if tensions remain into 2025. For investors who want to manage exposure to IRA Healthcare provisions (JPAMIRAH <Index>) and Child Tax Credit Beneficiaries (JPAMCTCB (<Index), see Figure 24 and Figure 26, respecitvely.

Congressional setup remains unclear but is unlikely to favor complete Democrat control. Republicans have a favorable electoral setup in the Senate given the narrow one seat majority for Democrats (i.e. 51-49). Specifically, Democrats need to defend 23 seats (includes 3 independents that caucus with the Dems), while Republicans only need to defend 10. The current map points to more vulnerable Democrat seats, including West Virginia, where Senator Manchin has decided not to seek re-election. Other vulnerable seats include Montana and Ohio, where both were previously carried by Trump in the 2020 Presidential Election. As it relates to the House, big swings in the number of seats controlled have been less volatile in the recent past during Presidential election years versus midterm election years. This suggests that a victory for either party will likely still result in narrow majorities for the winner. The slight hold that Republicans currently have in the House (i.e. 218-213 with 4 vacancies) means that the House would still be in play for both parties in this election cycle and perhaps even favoring Democrats given beneficial redistricting and lack of significant legislative achievements for Republicans. We find it interesting that since 1928, the party in control of the House after the midterm is usually the party that also controls the House after the subsequent Presidential election (exceptions are 1932, 1948, and 1952; see Figure 31). Given how tight majorities in the House are currently, it would not be surprising if 2024 is the first year that this incumbent control of the House does not hold during a Presidential election year.

Risk factors. We see US elections as one of several underappreciated policy risks in the second half of this year. Moreover, as investors grapple with the policy implications, it is important to keep in mind that the combined age of both candidates of 158 years with health concerns a very live risk. The inability of either frontrunner to be on the ballot in

November would create a tougher road to electoral victory for the Presidency. Ongoing legal cases/litigation present additional volatile headline risk in the run-up to election day. We also caution investors to scrutinize policy promises/implementation strategies, as they do not always translate into market outcomes. For example, coming into 2017, there were expectations of a strong pro-Energy agenda by the Republican administration across permitting reform, drilling on federal lands, etc. However, by the time they left office in 2021, Energy was down -30% and -113% relative to the benchmark (still -8% and -59%, respectively, pre-Covid). In short, other macro/idiosyncratic drivers still have large roles to play where policy is one of many inputs. In addition, national security priorities are increasingly factoring into economic decisions by both candidates, which presents new concerns around cross-border M&A (e.g. Nippon Steel/US Steel). This risk will likely persist as economic policy realigns with security policy. Moreover, unlike the last few administrations that have been able to execute large fiscal stimulus (e.g. TCJA, Covid-related spending), the next administration will likely be more limited in its ability to deficit spend given growing deficit consciousness (e.g. rising debt/GDP, deficit spending weighing on long-term economic growth) and political divisions even within one<sup>’</sup>s own party on the topic. Lastly, while markets have performed well during a period of split Congress (+11%/year, on average, for the S&P 500, congress has been split \~20% of the time since 1928), a split congress may not actually be desirable when key legislative provisions expire requiring action to renew or make permanent. For example, the expiring individual and certain business provisions of the TCJA, if not renewed, would result in a sizeable increase in taxes for most Americans and would cost \~\$3.5T if all provisions were made permanent through 2033 (see CBO). This is especially true when the controlling party has only a narrow majority and increasingly fractured unity across key issues.

Polling vs. Ballots. As discussed in the recent market volatility and commentary note (see Report), there has been much debate over the years on whether betting markets (including PredictIt, Smarkets, Betfair, Bovada) as well as their aggregators Electionbettingodds (volume weighted average of Betfair, PredictIt, Smarkets, and Polymarket) or RealClearPolitics (simple average of BetUS, Betsson, Bwin, PredictIt, SportingBet) have historically been more accurate than polls such as YouGov, Rasmussen Reports (and those e.g. collected on the 538 website) in predicting election outcomes. Despite their shortcomings, such as bans in certain countries, fees, potential vulnerability to manipulation, etc., academic research overall finds that prediction markets outperform polls, particularly earlier in the cycle. According to Berg et al. (2008), betting markets have been “closer to the eventual outcome 74% of the time<sup>”</sup> with “the market significantly outperform[ing] the polls<sup>”</sup> when forecasting “more than 100 days in advance.<sup>”</sup> Sethi et al. (2021) found that in swing states, markets data outperformed polls-based models from April (start of their analysis) until September 2020. This likely makes prediction markets relevant to investors now and into the summer. According to aggregator Election betting odds, over \$400 million was bet on the 2020 election, with volumes increasing over the past elections.

Presidential Betting Odds and Polls Tightening. Betting odds point to a dead heat between the candidates, though polling still favors Republicans, especially in swing states. Looking at RCP average betting odds, both candidates are currently separated by just \~1.5 points, favoring Republicans slightly (43.4 vs. 42.3, see Figure 5). This has been a dramatic shift given the near-20-point lead the Republican challenger held just in mid-February. Some betting sites such as PredictIt actually have the Democrat candidate leading, though the wider margin just a few weeks back has meaningfully narrowed (50.0 vs. 47.0, see Figure 4). Though not as extreme, RCP

average polling still narrowly favors the Republican candidate (46.6 vs. 45.1), with the spread widening since mid-April (see Figure 6). It<sup>’</sup>s worth keeping in mind that the RCP polling showed the current Democratic incumbent ahead at all points in time during the 2020 election (see Figure 7).

Swing States Still Favor Republicans. Very narrow voting blocs across key swing states (as the name implies) have driven electoral outcomes in the last two elections and should be followed closely as we near the election. Currently, average swing state polling (e.g. Michigan, Wisconsin, Pennsylvania, Arizona, Nevada and Georgia) favors the Republican candidate by 2.6 points, though that margin has been shrinking (4.0 three months ago). Given the Electoral College, Presidential elections in the US are settled by thousands of votes as opposed to millions (see Report). For example, the 2020 election was incredibly close despite the electoral count (306 vs. 232) and popular vote (51.3% vs. 46.9%) margins would lead one to believe. In fact, a shift of just \~44K votes in 3 key swing states (Arizona, Georgia and Wisconsin) would have thwarted a Democrat Presidential victory. Similarly, in the 2016 election the Republican challenger won the electoral count (304 vs. 227, accounts for faithless electors) and lost the popular vote (46.2% to 48.2%). That being said, a \~78K vote shift across 3 swing states (Michigan, Wisconsin and Pennsylvania) would have led to a Democratic candidate victory.

Macro implications. Our Derivatives strategists view elections as a source of equity volatility. Specifically, in the past 50 years, S&P 500 realized volatility was \~2 points higher in a US election year than in non-election years (see Report). Moreover, while it is still more than 6 months out, options markets are pricing in a material risk premium around the US elections in November, illustrated by the kink in the term structure (see Report). The options market is currently implying a \~2.8% move on the S&P 500 following the election, which is moderately above the average 2.1% move the market recorded on the trading session following election days so far in the 21st century. Unlike in the run up to the 2020 election, the option market is (so far) not pricing in a meaningful risk of a contested election result, though in 2020 this only began to emerge in mid-July. Our Systematic/Quant Strategists similarly highlight how VIX peaked and SPX fell around 3 business days before the election day, as people seek protections and shy away from risky assets due to the uncertainty from the US presidential election (see Report). Post election, VIX came back to the level/trend before the event, and SPX started rallying. In the 2016 and 2020 elections, SPX index was up by 4.6% and 6.6%, respectively, in the 30 business days window we investigated. If market conditions are similar to those in 2016/2020, they think the dispersion trade should perform during the 2024 presidential election period. Our FX strategists expect upside risks to USD in the lead-up to the election. Policy transmission through trade/tariffs, US-China relations, fiscal policy and currency policy have the potential to be dollar positive. Tariffs are likely the dominant channel of FX risk premium (see Report). Immigration remains a key issue of concern for both candidates (see Report). While the economic impacts to inflation remain unclear, any effective efforts to restrict immigration could be a potential driver of inflation if it creates significant labor shortfalls, thereby curbing growth.

Sector performance around elections. On average/median, the S&P 500 has been positive and outperforming non-election years 3-6Ms before election day going back to 1932 (see Figure 32S&P 50erfomanc (i)B dAt sl E). The day after election day has typically been negative, but the trend has been bucked in the past two election cycles (i.e. 2016 and 2020). As expected, we see more dispersion at the sector/industry levels. For example, since 1992, we have observed fairly consistent (7 out of 8) relative outperformance for S&P 500 Industrials (+2.7% on avg vs. market) and Financials (+3.3%) 3Ms before election day. Healthcare has consistently outperformed the day after the election (+1.6%), and Industrials have usually been strong relative outperformers 1M after the election (+1.5%). Coming out of the election, sectors such as Staples (9M, -5.5%) and Communication Services (12M, - 6.6%) have been consistent relative underperformers.

Relative performance into the election. At the industry level, Food/Staples Retailing (+7.9) and Software Services (+1.3%) have, on average, been relative outperformers consistently 9Ms before election day since 1990. Consumer Services have performed well (+3.5%) 3Ms in advance, along with Banks, which have performed well at both the 3M and 1M mark heading into the election (+5.2% and +2.3%, respectively). Transportation companies have tended to do well 1M before election day (+4.6%), having outperformed the index in all periods back to 1992 except in 2020 (likely due to the pandemic). Interestingly, we observe consistent relative underperformance by Autos/Components 6Ms and 9Ms before elections. The consistent beneficiary the day after the election has tended to be Pharma/ Biotech/Life Sciences (+1.9%), likely given election sensitivities around changes in healthcare policy/regulation.

Relative performance after the election. At the industry level, performance 1M after the election has been typically mixed/inconsistent. After 3Ms, Diversified Financials (+1.4%) have been consistent relative outperformers. We see more bifurcation at the 9M mark, where Insurance has been a relative outperformer (+5.7%) and Telecom Services a relative underperformer (-6.7%), on average, versus the benchmark. Finally, 12Ms after election day, Financials have been the only consistent outperformer (7 out of 8, +8.8%), with the only relative underperformance during the 2008 financial crisis (-16.4%).

## CHIPS Act Beneficiaries (JPAMCHIP)

Our JPM stock analysts identified US companies likely to benefit from provisions within the recently passed CHIPS Act. These companies are expected to benefit from the multi-year spending outlays towards manufacturing incentives R&D within the bill. Constituents are heavily tilted towards the Semiconductor and Life Sciences Tools/Services industries.

## Energy Companies Most Sensitive to Geopolitics (JPAMGNRG)

Our JPM stock analysts identified US companies sensitive to rising geopolitical tensions, especially as it relates to Iran. A hawkish Iran policy could drive geopolitical risk and thereby higher oil prices. In effect, this would be good for oil but bad for the economy/growth.

## Child Tax Credit Beneficiaries (JPAMCTCB)

The Tax Relief for American Families and Workers Act of 2024 includes provisions for Child Tax Credit expansion. Our JPM stock analysts identified US companies likely to benefit from the expansion of child tax credits under the existing proposal or future legislation. These companies are heavily tilted towards consumer sectors.

## Corporate and News Sentiment

News Sentiment on US Politics and Trade Policy is starting to decline ahead of US elections. Corporates are also beginning to express concerns and negative sentiment from the uncertainty around trade policies.

The Sentiment data is drawn from SmartBuzz tool. SmartBuzz is an NLP processing framework that tracks market themes using a taxonomy curated to capture market relevant topics with the help of JPM’s Strategists and AI. We combine sentiment analysis with the theme exposure of stocks. The news feed is curated from LexisNexis and Corporate Transcripts from S&P/CapIQ.

## UK Equity Strategy

Prabhav Bhadani, CFA prabhav.bhadani@jpmorgan.com J.P. Morgan Securities plc

Figure 46: FTSE 100 price relative since 2016
![](images/bfa8bb46421b36e535d67a123eea8e3fafd5783e70451f1e4531ffdfe8be362c.jpg)
Source: Datastream

The UK has weathered an extended period of political uncertainty since the Brexit vote back in 2016. Rishi Sunak, the current prime minister, is the third Tory leader to lead the UK government in last couple of years.

Figure 47: UK General Election Polling
![](images/1007a0fc6de75c84ea2163090332cc742794c4df61f8a999742dd32c1aa5f120.jpg)
Source: Various Polls, J.P. Morgan

The next UK parliament must take place before the 28<sup>th</sup> of January, 2025. The general consensus is that the elections will be held this autumn, and some even suggest that it could take place sooner during the summer months. As things stand, the Labour party has a sizeable lead over the incumbent Conservatives. Polls point to roughly 43% of respondents backing Labour versus only 23% favouring the Conservatives.

Figure 48: UK General Election Polling Spread: Labour - Conservative
![](images/7d08b3942c96ff2fe3044a9276f459fe6d99f91f4f51679a6eaacaa8837766b0.jpg)

This is the widest spread in polling intentions between the two main parties going back to the 1930s. While the Brexit vote and the 2019 elections when Theresa May<sup>’</sup>s (Conservative party leader at the time) sizeable lead vanished over a period of 6 weeks serve as reminders that polls can shift, we do not think such an outcome is likely this time around. Labour leader Keir Starmer has been adopting a risk-averse strategy and is staying tight lipped on specific policy details, and recent efforts by the Conservatives to better differentiate them from Labour have had no visible impact.

Currently, the Labour party is well placed to win the next UK elections. We believe markets will respond positively to this outcome, even though historically Labour governments have received a relatively tepid response from the market in the first few weeks in office.

## Labour versus Conservative policies

In the last elections, held in 2019, the general consensus was that a Labour victory would be a negative for the overall equity market. There were concerns around nationalization of key industries and also the possibility of higher taxes.

In our assessment, the current Labour leadership under Kier Starmer is much more centrist than the manifesto going into the 2019 elections would suggest. They have already distanced themselves from the Corbyn Era nationalization promises concerning the Utility sector and have suggested much more fiscal discipline. Their promise of nationalizing the railways isn<sup>’</sup>t likely to be fiscally draining and enjoys broad public support. We acknowledge that there is still some way to go before the next elections, and there has been very limited communication from the Labour party on their agenda thus far.

We believe both Conservatives and Labour will be fiscally constrained over the coming years and may even need to tighten fiscal policy / raise some taxes. See report from our economists here. Within EU, UK debt grew the fastest only after France since the onset of Covid. A Labour government will probably focus on using supply side levers to help improve economic growth.

Under a Labour government, we would likely see increased healthcare spend on the NHS and continued support for households on gas and electricity bills. These measures would likely be paid for by raising taxes on private schools and money from windfall taxes on oil and gas companies.

A Labour victory would likely be seen as a negative for the Oil and Gas sector. The Tories have been surprisingly aggressive in taxing the industry and have last week extended the windfall taxes by another year to 2029. The Energy profits levy (EPL) is currently charged on profits at 35% as a part of a headline tax rate of 75%. The Labour party proposals broadly involve raising this tax, reducing tax reliefs, and extending the period for which the levy applies beyond 2029. A factor to consider here is that the fossil fuel industry is a big employer, particularly in Scotland, where Labour is looking to win seats from the SNP. The unions here are very strong and clearly have a lot of influence on the Labour Party.

The current government has set a target of delivering “net zero<sup>”</sup> power by 2035. Labour<sup>’</sup>s ambition is bolder still, with a target of achieving clean power by 2030. The plan involves major investments in on-shore and off-shore wind farms and more investment in nuclear and green hydrogen.

Recent Labour communication has championed the idea of Great British Energy. The plan is to make the supply of energy in the UK more cost competitive by introducing a state funded player. They have also projected Great British Energy as a vehicle to expedite research and development of new technologies in the production and distribution of power. We see this as increasing the competitive landscape for the existing players and believe it would be a negative for the listed names.

Labour has promised to reinstate the 2030 ban on sale of new petrol and diesel cars and vans after the government announced it would delay it till 2035. Our sector specialists see limited impact from this change on the broader sector.

A key point of differentiation for Labour could be a renewed focus on supply of affordable housing. They have suggested cutting red tape to expedite the approval process for new-builds (including reform to “Not in my backyard<sup>”</sup> policy). This would be a positive for home builders, especially the ones geared towards the lower end of the housing market.

On Defence, both Labour and Conservatives have very similar stance. Recent Labour communication suggests that they support higher Defence spending.

The nationalization of railways would be a negative for the listed players, including Firstgroup and Trainline.

## Potential market implication

We believe a Labour victory in the next UK election is likely to drive positive market reaction. While there are some industries like Energy, Transportation and Utilities where Labour<sup>’</sup>s policies would be a drag and potentially higher wage costs might be a drag for retail and hospitality, for the broader market, political and economic stability and the focus of improving supply side dynamics would be a support.

## Key sector views

## Utilities – Negative

We expect that a Labour government would be supportive of electricity networks and renewables. Given the target to decarbonise the power grid by 2030, we expect support for accelerated networks capex, which would benefit National Grid and SSE the most. Offshore wind development in the UK should also be supported, which would favour companies including Orsted, RWE, SSE, iberdrola, Engie and EDPR. We note that there could be downside pressure, depending on the execution of Labour<sup>’</sup>s plans for state-owned utility “GB Energy<sup>”</sup>. Finally, we expect that a Labour government will continue to push for improvements in the water sector, with pressure remaining high on both the companies and its regulators. We see the water stocks, United Utilities, Severn Trent, and Pennon, as stocks to avoid on this dynamic.

## Energy – Negative

Labour has indicated that they intend to increase and extend the Energy Profits Levy and even reduce some of the investment-related offsets. This, if implemented, would be negative for the sector. Overall exposure of major E&P players is relatively limited to the UK market. We believe Enquest and Harbour Energy are vulnerable to such announcements.

## Banks – Positive

In contrast to the 2019 elections, when the Labour manifesto included higher taxes and a consultation into the breakup of RBS, a Labour victory might actually be a positive for the sector. Political and policy stability would be seen as supportive for the broader sector.

## Home builders – Positive

Labour is likely to be focused on affordable housing and unlocking land for development. This would likely be quite supportive for home builders exposed to the lower end of the property market. In particular, we highlight Persimmon as a likely beneficiary.

## Healthcare – No impact

Despite potentially higher spending on NHS by Labour, we see limited impact on the private players given that the NHS is operating over capacity.

## Food retail – Modest positive

Bearing in mind that grocery elasticity is not as material as in other categories, directionally, a potential Labour victory could result in a positive push for the sector. The party<sup>’</sup>s focus on reducing energy bills through gaining energy security and creation of clean energy projects could help food retailers in managing a large chunk of costs associated with utilities (currently c2% of revenues, which nearly doubled during the crises). On the consumer side, these projects and further private investments into creating industrial jobs could help purchasing power, allowing more opportunity to trade up, eventually leading to an uptick in real volumes and the overall retailers<sup>’</sup> top lines.

Higher wage costs may be a negative.

## Transportation – Negative

Nationalization of the railways would weigh on the sector. The initial reaction of stocks like Trainline and Firstgroup has indeed been negative. However, on Trainline, our covering analysts believe that the UK Labour policy on rail could be a net positive for the investment case – removing uncertainty and political risk through 2024 – and argue that regulatory changes around Pay As You Go ticketing, commission rates as well as ongoing industry strike action are manageable. Importantly, they highlight that the proposed measures showed a commitment to deliver simplified fares and ticketing, while rolling out innovations aligned with those previously trialed by Trainline – digital season tickets and automatic delay/repay, along with an expected commitment to establish Great British Railways – though not revisiting the notion of a central retailing app – while at the same time re-nationalise currently franchised train operators within five years. While they recognize headline risk ahead, with shares historically volatile through the run into elections, they stay constructive on the long-term narrative and upside opportunity on rising online share in the UK and compelling aggregation proposition through carrier fragmentation in Europe.

## India Equity Strategy

India<sup>’</sup>s national elections commenced on April 19 and will be conducted in seven phases till 1 June. The 44-day-long electoral process will conclude with the counting of the votes on 4 June. There are 968 million eligible voters in 2024, with 18 million voters in the 18-19 age group who will be eligible to vote for the first time. The elections pit the ruling National Democratic Alliance (NDA), led by Prime Minister Narendra Modi – who is aiming for a third term – against the opposition INDIA (Indian National Developmental Inclusive Alliance). Historically heading into elections, we find: (1) as elections get going, the market goes through an extended period of elevated volatility, that declines sharply post conclusion; (2) Nifty has delivered +9%/+8% in the 3/6 months post general elections since 1991, which means any correction or dip usually ends up as a buying opportunity. The options market is currently anticipating a move of +3.1% in Nifty on 2024 election results. The current implied move is lower than in previous election years, suggesting that the options market perceives the risk of a political surprise as relatively low.

Figure 54: Nifty implied volatility tends to rise around start of elections and fall on conclusion
NSE Volatility Index, days around conclusion of national election
![](images/3c98869bf08488e9c90e1ea2e2a2f0a8b4e70afab0e6e618c77c4402af65b95b.jpg)
Source: J.P.Morgan, Bloomberg Finance L.P.

Based on latest opinion polls, BJP-led NDA’s seat sharing projection has increased to close to 380+. Key states to watch will be Uttar Pradesh, Maharashtra, West Bengal and Bihar, which send 210 of 543 members of parliament to the Lok Sabha. In the last general election in 2019, the BJP secured the highest 303 seats, followed by the Indian National Congress (INC) with 52 seats. As a precursor to the general elections, five states<sup>’</sup> assembly elections results in December 2023 provided a big boost to the NDA. The interim budget also signaled BJP<sup>’</sup>s confidence ahead of elections. The government desisted from increases in social schemes, and fiscal consolidation was the overarching theme.

The current government has undertaken several reforms, ranging from well-known ones like the GST, the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) and Real Estate Regulatory Act (RERA) to lesser-known ones like abolishing the “small scale reservations list<sup>”</sup> for manufacturing. Strong GDP growth is a reflection of the structural reforms over the last 10 years. The BJP manifesto for the third term is focusing on continuity, policy stability and reforms in all factors of production to boost manufacturing, increasing control on supply chains and creating jobs. The Congress manifesto focuses on social justice, job creation (and filling) and nation-wide caste census.

We expect broad economic and fiscal policy continuity to be most likely outcome. Government will likely continue to prioritize infrastructure spending, affordable housing, reforms in all factors of production to boost manufacturing, potentially increasing the country<sup>’</sup>s control on supply chains and creating jobs. Our Nifty-50 Index target is 22,000. If BJP retains power in the 2024 elections with a higher-than-expected seats share and RBI starts easing amidst a potentially benign US/global macro outcome (“goldilocks<sup>”</sup>), then the Nifty may trade between our base and bull case (25000) target led by a further P/E re-rating. We believe real estate, EMS and capex recovery plays like industrials, utilities, cement, auto and banks will be key beneficiaries. However, if the general election results are unexpected, along with a global recession, geopolitical tensions, higher oil prices, or higher domestic unemployment, it could lead to a period of uncertainty with the Nifty trading close to our bear case (16000). Five long-term themes to focus on: 1) Indigenous Defense, 2) Savers to Investors, 3) Waterways, 4) Affordable Housing and 5) Manufacturing Renaissance (link).

## Mexico Equity Strategy

Mexico<sup>’</sup>s 2024 general election (to be held on June 2nd) will be the largest in the country<sup>’</sup>s history, and its importance cannot be understated. The incoming President will serve a six-year term, while all seats in both chambers of Congress, nine governorships and 30 local congresses will be renovated. Although we believe the election is unlikely to be a big market mover, there is some room for positive surprises should the race be closer than what current polls are showing. However, with the incumbent polling at around 60%, on average (\~30pp gap to the opposition), all eyes are on whether it will be able to reach the two-thirds majority in Congress it requires to pass constitutional reforms, which we believe is not currently priced in and implies downside risk. Importantly, however, recent polls show the opposition coalition gaining some momentum at the state level, with Mexico City a very close call, which ultimately has an important effect on Congress composition. In addition, as is the case every 12 years, Mexico<sup>’</sup>s election cycle coincides with the US<sup>’</sup>s, whose results, and its read-through towards Mexico, seems to be of far greater concern for investors than the domestic story. Still, regardless of the outcome of either one of these races, key addressable topics for the next administration will be: (1) the near-shoring opportunity at the federal level and its bottlenecks, in particular related to energy policy; (2) potential for fiscal reform given pressure on public federal spending; (3) foreign policy, in particular with regards to USMCA renegotiations in 2026 and ongoing panels; and (4) security.

0 Not looking at an austere government, and the need for a fiscal reform is imminent. All candidates have remained tilted towards populist messages in an effort to scoop votes. The incumbent (Claudia Sheinbaum, Morena) continues to emphasize there is no urge to implement a fiscal reform even if her agenda is close to the President<sup>’</sup>s policy pillars: heavy social spending at the cost of reducing capital spending, loosely counterbalanced by a revenue policy that so far has been rooted in exhausting public trust, contingency (rainy-day) funds and maximizing tax efficiencies to avoid increasing taxes. Our economists believe that the outlook moving forward points to the need for a fiscal reform, as failure to arrest some of the most troublesome trends (particularly with regards to the increase in the debt ratio and several spending components) in the next few years could lead to a rating downgrade.

NoFTA risks back into focus. Recently proposed reforms threaten to amplify existing tensions in the US-Mexico relationship, which have been affected by the migratory crisis, little progress against organized crime, and increased involvement of Chinese investment in Mexico, particularly in the all-important auto sector. With elections in both countries approaching fast, the narrative on these topics is likely to move to the center stage soon. Moreover, regardless of the outcome of either election, 2H26 is set to have the first review of the USMCA, and it is expected that candidates will start to ask questions on the slow progress on recent disputes (energy and GMO corn) and the impact of AMLO<sup>’</sup>s proposed reforms. This uncertainty could deter new investments, despite recent uptick in FDI, in deterrence of the huge boost the US has made to relocate projects away from China to the US-Mexico border manufacturing belt. Lastly, the prospect of a Trump presidency weighs heavily over Mexico. Rhetoric between the US and Mexico throughout the election cycle is expected to be hotter than usual, especially among Republicans, many of whom have been very critical on Mexico<sup>’</sup>s handling of the aforementioned issues and argue they create threats to the United States (migration, organized crime and supply chain relocation).

Nearshoring, nearshoring, nearshoring (and energy). Addressing the nearshoring opportunity with a federal level strategy is probably the biggest low-hanging fruit the next administration has to ensure continued growth. However, strategies differ greatly between the incumbent and the opposition, with the former likely to focus most efforts on developing the south of the country through the continuation of AMLO<sup>’</sup>s flagship projects (the Mayan train, the Dos Bocas refinery and the Isthmus of Tehuantepec). Bottlenecks in need of addressing include access to utilities for relocating companies, particularly water and electricity. On the latter, both candidates have expressed their desire to boost a sustainable energy transformation, but the opposition (Xochitl Galvez) is far more likely to allow private investment to retake its role in the sector. In addition, Ms. Galvez has expressed her administration would focus on the construction of transmission and distribution lines of high, medium and low voltage (which have not grown in the past several years) and possibly restructuring Pemex. Names to play the nearshoring thematic in Mexico include Industrial Real Estate (FIBRAPL, VESTA\*, TERRA13, FIBRAMQ, FUNO11), followed by Airports (OMAB and GAPB), and some Banks and Staples names with high northern exposure (namely RA and AC\*).

What about market performance? Mexican equities tend to trade sideways in the six months prior to a general election, with average returns at +1% (+3% in LC) in the past four cycles. As expected, performance post-election is largely related to the outcome, with the market posting mid-teens returns following positive surprises but down around the same level when negative. Average returns in the six months following an election have been +5% (+2% in LC). We provide a list of 10 OW- or N-rated names that have historically outperformed the market in the six months following an election, regardless of the outcome: AC\*, ASURB, RA, LIVEPOLC, GAPB, GFNORTEO, KOF, MEGACPO, KIMBERA and WALMEX\*. Our analysis includes tests in each of the four prior presidential election cycles: 2000, 2006, 2012 and 2018.

## South Africa Equity Strategy

Bottom-up view suggests minimal policy risk; 24% upside from a better-thanexpected political outcome

The 29 May elections/June potential coalition formation process are much discussed by SA investors, but we see little (c 2-4%) downside risk priced into SA equities. On a bottom-up sector basis, we see few policy risks. Right now we see the ANC likely to achieve 45% – as best we can tell from the small number of high variance polls; we will update views as we get new polling data. That puts a lot of market uncertainty into the formation of a post-election coalition, which on these numbers seems the most likely outcome. We see upside risks too – an investor-friendly coalition could spark a rally, like the 24%/two-month rally we saw in the Dec <sup>‘</sup>17-Feb <sup>‘</sup>18 Ramaphoria. Our key OW sectors on the JSE include General Miners and Gold among the offshore sectors and Banks and Staples among the domestic sectors; our key UWs are General Retail and Food Producers. We are structurally UW South Africa in our CEEMEA and EM country allocations until we see the “two twos<sup>”</sup>: two years of >2% GDP growth, which should trigger a domestic re-rating and better earnings growth. However, near term, we see SA equities as oversold and likely to bounce ahead of elections.

Bottom up, while investors may be concerned about potential policy changes in a coalition government, most of our analysts believe policy risk is fairly benign. Our analysts highlight the top three risks in a more populist-leaning coalition government to be: 1) National Health Insurance (NHI), although the consensus view is that it<sup>’</sup>s not likely to be implemented; 2) increased taxes and royalties, which could affect key industries such as mining and diversified chemicals; and 3) a possible drive to increase locally manufactured merchandise, which would change the retailers<sup>’</sup> costs structures if implemented. Interestingly, land expropriation was not flagged as a meaningful risk, likely due to the high hurdle required for a constitutional amendment. We have ranked FY24e HEPS sensitivity to 1% ZAR weakness, showing miners are most positively impacted, and retailers most negatively impacted.

When we look at valuations, either SA’s discount to EM (20%) or the AllShare ex offshore sectors (x 12m fwd), we see we are bumping along at the same low valuations we have seen in the last 3+ years; this implies little election risk is priced in. We expect – as usual – much of the SA-specific risks will play out through the FX rate: the cliché “low vol All Share vs high vol ZAR<sup>”</sup>. Our EM FICC strategy team also sees little election risk priced into the currency/rates/spreads. However, outside of elections, SA equities could surprise to the upside and re-rate if the Fed cuts sooner than investors expect or commodities run harder. Domestically, a faster resolution to the infrastructure issues could also spark a rally.

See our detailed report, SA Election Outlook – stocks price in 5 more years of ANC (link).

## Emerging Markets

2024 US presidential elections could exert gravitational power on EM equities. We discuss multiple angles of impact in this report, such as the co-movement of EM and US equities, focus on important 2024 EM elections, debate on the US fiscal dynamics that could be mimicked in EM, and USD directionality as a driver for EM stocks. We identify the stocks in major EMs that are the most correlated to strength / weakness in currency (Table 3).

● Can EM flourish amid weaker US? US and EM equities move together most of the time. 83% of the time US and EM equities have produced monthly returns in the same direction. A lasting disconnect between US and EM monthly returns is a tail risk looking backward. And, as expected, EM produced nearly 1.4% more/less return vs US equities in good/bad months.

The US elections might bring focus also on important 2024 EM elections. We have long held the view that the outlook for EM equities cannot be dissociated from political regimes. There are major elections lined up across EM in 2024: Mexico, India, South Africa, and Egypt. Our FX strategists note that politics should feature prominently for FX into 2024, with US elections as the headline event risk. The incoming government sets the tone for re/de-rating risk for equities via macroeconomic policies. Sluggish growth combined with high debt burden creates a breeding ground for populist narratives. De-globalization decisions could go hand in hand with populist agendas and inflate equity risk premia.

Intra-EM rotation opportunities. Table 3 highlights the top five names from the eight largest EM markets that have historically shown the most positive and negative correlations with their respective local currencies. Positive correlation with stronger local FX is higher in some markets (Brazil, Mexico, South Africa) and lower in others (Saudi, due to USD peg). Financials stocks are the most positively correlated with strengthening local currency. Exporters (margin expansion) and defensive sectors (hedge for risk) were most prevalent among stocks positively correlated with weaker local currencies.

● Keep an eye on twin-deficit economies. Fiscal deficits in most EMs are running wider than in 2019 as a legacy of the pandemic. Given elevated cost of long-term government debt, reducing future deficits would require stringent measures. In 2024, markets that should present twin deficits >2% of GDP are South Africa, Turkey, Egypt, Chile, and Colombia.

Back to basics on fiscal discipline. Growth shortfall and higher borrowing costs could challenge fiscal discipline and worsen debt sustainability. Most of EM is poised to narrow fiscal deficits into next year, even as aggregate growth is forecast to slow slightly. J.P. Morgan forecasts the aggregate EM fiscal deficit to decline to - 4.2% in 2024 from -4.4% last year.

## US Equity Gravity

2024 US presidential elections could exert gravitational power on EM equities. Many angles are discussed in this section, such as the co-movement of EM and US equities, the focus also on important 2024 EM elections, debate on the US fiscal dynamics that could be mimicked in EM, and USD directionality as a driver for EM stocks. Our FX strategists note that politics should feature prominently for FX into 2024, with US elections as the headline event risk.

## Can EM Flourish amid Weaker US?

Since 2000, US and EM equities have produced monthly returns in the same direction for 83% of the time (Figure 68 . A lasting disconnect between US and EM monthly returns is a tail risk looking backward. And, as expected, EM produced nearly 1.4% more/less returns vs US equities in good/bad months.

US and EM equities moving up. (1) Poland, Brazil, Turkey, and Hungary presented average outperformance to EM equities greater than 1ppt in more than 50% of the months of positive US/EM equity returns; (2) Energy, IT, Materials, and Financials outperformed EM in more than 50% of months of positive US/EM returns, but the average outperformance was marginal (less than 1%).

US and EM equities moving down. (1) Egypt, Malaysia, Philippines, Indonesia, Peru, and Thailand presented average outperformance to EM equities by greater than 1ppt in more than 50% of the months of negative US/EM equity returns; (2) Healthcare, Consumer Staples, and Utilities had average outperformance of greater than 1ppt in more than 50% of the months of negative US/EM equity return.

## US and EM 2024 Elections

The US elections might bring focus also on important 2024 EM elections. We have long held the view that the outlook for EM equities cannot be dissociated from political regimes. The relevance lies in an administration<sup>’</sup>s willingness and ability to embrace policies that result in sustainable growth.

The incoming government sets the tone for re/de-rating risk for equities by: (1) polishing macroeconomic pillars – floating FX, fiscal discipline, and inflation targeting; (2) facing the need for structural reforms to enhance competitiveness, such as to simplify an onerous tax system,decrease labor costs, invest in infrastructure; and (3) strike the right balance between previous objectives and social spending/ campaign promises.

Keep an eye on elections across EM: Mexico, India, and South Africa (Table 2). While macro responsible policies are necessary to sustain higher GDP growth in the long run, political leaders must precisely calculate the tipping point where “lack of growth<sup>”</sup> erodes political capital to make the first objective sustainable.

Keep an eye on twin-deficit economies. Given elevated costs of long-term government debt, reducing future deficits would require stringent measures such as expenditure cuts, higher taxes, and divestment of government assets. By the end of 2024, markets that should present twin deficits >2% of GDP are South Africa, Turkey and Egypt in CEEMEA and Chile and Colombia in LatAm (Table 4).

De-globalization decisions can go hand in hand with populist decision-making and inflate equity risk premia: (1) Lower trade intensity would have a negative impact on growth, and the breakdown of trade and technology flows could raise prices. (2) Emergence of “thicker<sup>”</sup> business borders could command fragmented supply chains and challenge operating leverage and economies of scale as drivers of EPS growth; (3) Geopolitical tensions in the last few years have already set in motion a supply chain relocation. Although there are some winners from shifting away of supply chains from strategic locations, in the bigger picture global growth is dented. For more, please refer to The great supply chain relocation: ASEANs rise, India<sup>’</sup>s potential, USMCA and Chino-Latino flows, by Joyce Chang and team.

## Back to Basics on Fiscal

Growth shortfall and higher borrowing costs could challenge fiscal discipline and worsen debt sustainability. Given the uncertain environment, governments need to prudently manage finances so as to tackle emerging contingencies efficiently and at the same time address debt vulnerabilities.

Fiscal deficits in most EMs are running wider than 2019 levels as a legacy of the pandemic. Market participants are attentive to fiscal slippages. There is no room for mistakes to turn temporary aid into permanent support. EM borrowed from the future, and this might have adverse consequences to potential GDP and EPS growth.

Most of EM is poised to narrow fiscal deficits this year, even as aggregate growth is forecast to slow slightly. J.P. Morgan forecasts the aggregate fiscal deficit as a % of GDP for emerging markets to decline to -4.1% in 2024 from -4.3% last year.

JPM expects EM fiscal thrust to remain negative in 2024 (-0.2%) but decline in magnitude versus 2023 (-0.5%). Fiscal consolidation should help central banks to anchor inflation expectations. Inflation already inched closer to central bank target range across EM last year and is forecast to decline sequentially through 2024,

allowing EM monetary policy to remain dovish.

EM gross government leverage continues to be dominated by domestic debt. Most EM countries have a higher proportion of domestic government debt as compared to external, and this trend has increased in the past year.

## Stock Ideas for USD Strength / Weakness

Intra-EM rotation opportunities. We run a correlation analysis of the top 60 stocks with their respective USD-local currencies. In Table 3 we highlight the top five companies from the eight largest EM markets that have historically (last five years) shown the most positive and negative correlations with their respective home currencies.

Markets. As expected, a large majority of stocks have positive correlation with strengthening currencies, implying that as local currencies strengthen, these stocks usually observe an upward movement. The magnitude of the correlation with stronger local FX is higher in some markets (Brazil, Mexico, South Africa) while relatively lower in other markets (Saudi Arabia, due to USD peg).

Sectors. Financial stocks are the most positively correlated with strengthening in their respective local currencies. On the other hand, the stocks positively correlated to weaker currencies majorly include exporters (margin expansion) and defensive sectors (hedge for risk).

A compass to steer direction for stocks, not a precise ruler. Stock correlations to USD-local currencies in these markets are low (below 55% for all). This means this exercise should be taken as a directional compass, rather than a precise ruler.

## US Elections & USD Performance

We present below some excerpts from our currency strategy report US Elections & FX: Mapping transmission from potential policies to USD, by Patrick Locke and team, published on 27 November 2023.

Risks to the dollar are skewed bullishly heading into elections if current polling stays intact. We offer a framework for thinking through several channels that will impact FX and USD: (1) trade policy / tariffs, (2) US-China relations, (3) fiscal policy, and currency policy.

We estimate 4-6% USD appreciation if universal 10% tariffs were to be introduced. Polls show that Trump continues to maintain a wide lead for the Republican nomination, increasing the odds of a 2020 rematch vs Joe Biden. FX will be predisposed to focus on tariffs, and not just because of deja vu. Tariffs will likely feature as a prominent issue this election cycle. The White House can act largely unilaterally on this front, which means renewed threats (including a 10% universal tariff on all imports are generally not checked by Congressional constraints and so can be enacted relatively quickly). This biases pricing of FX risk premia more toward binary outcomes around tariffs more so than fiscal for 2024.

CNY should still net weaken in the event additional tariffs are imposed, but the exante setup is different now vs 2018 as (1) the entry point is different now – the shock value of trade conflict is diminished vs 2018, and China still carries a 20% effective tariff rate on exports to the US; (2) the tariff-rate regime governing USD/CNY in 2018- 2019 has since evolved despite no tariff relief; and (3) positioning and valuations are more supportive now for CNY, while foreigners have liquidated positions at a nearrecord pace this year.

Broadening of the strategic US-China conflict is asymmetrically dollar positive via anti-cyclical channels. Strategic rivalry with China is entrenched regardless of party, and trade represents just one of multiple fronts. We apply our USD TWI cyclical models to highlight that, via major global growth drags from a broadening of the conflict, USD would could see up to +6% TWI appreciation via its anti-cyclical supports, with more strength from the tech war and the weaponization of financials than the trade conflict.

Fiscal policy has been a meaningful support for USD across the last two administrations, but we see less scope for transformative policy impacting the dollar this cycle. Medium-term risks around the deficit will persist for USD, though a reversion lower is not necessarily imminent.

An objective assessment highlights EUR, MXN, and select EM Asia as potentially at risk in a new tariff regime. We juxtapose the prospects for tariffs and rank tactical considerations across positioning, valuations, and potential economic hit. MXN, EUR, and select EM Asia all show meaningful trade balances with the US, with some mix of risks. Nothing screens off the charts, however, and we are cognizant of other fundamentals like MXN that are markedly improved from 2016. Still, this can be used to gauge which economies may be more subject to renewed tariff risks.

What lessons from the 2018-19 trade conflict are still relevant? FX was uniquely responsive to tariffs and the trade conflict. Risk premia in the election cycle can emerge as market participants revisit the direct and indirect impacts on FX.

With tariffs, FX is impacted first and foremost via competitiveness adjustments. CNY depreciated as a direct and significant offset to tariffs in 2018- 2019, which all else equal made Chinese goods less competitive. USD/CNY moved +5% for every 10% increase in the effective rate on China.

The economic spillover channel was a clear dollar positive, and more so against cyclical currencies. Our economists have noted the rather muted direct GDP drag from tariffs but also point out that the indirect spillover took global growth down by -0.5% in 2019. That was worth approximately +2% in the USD TWI at the time.

USD strength was ultimately concentrated more in higher-beta FX because of the growth drag, particularly those with high betas to USD/CNY (CNY pairwise correlations reached all-time highs in 2019).

Tariffs never really went away. Those hoping for broad based tariff relief under the current administration have been disappointed. It is unlikely that any “peace dividend<sup>”</sup> here will arise that can sponsor material USD weakness
